## Pagina 53

2025 CHAMPIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE




Febvre had been strong in the 2024 campaign when
present, but a thumb injury at his home GP derailed
his title challenge just when he was catching fire,
and he was unable to compete for the whole year,
eventually claiming a single race win in China on
his way to fifth in the series. Of course, the veteran
Frenchman has been considered a contender for
every MXGP title since he surprised many by claiming
the crown in his very first year in the class. Back
in 2015, much of the pre-season hype had been
focussed on the upcoming battle between multiple
Champion Antonio Cairoli and the incoming American
legend Ryan Villopoto. Few eyes were on the French
rookie with the MX2 bronze medal around his neck
and just a single GP win to his name.

In what was a particularly tough year, however,
injuries to both the pre-season favourites, as well
as to further contenders such as Max Nagl and
Clement Desalle, brought the Yamaha man into focus,
breaking through with a famous first MXGP victory on
home soil at Villars-sous-Ecot, the first of 8 GP wins
in the final 11 rounds of the series. He took the title a
round early, in Mexico, and completed his joy with a
win at the final round in California plus a dominating
performance as part of a victorious Team France at
the Motocross of Nations at Ernée!

Since then, injuries have kept him away from the
crown when he often looked to be in contention.
Engaged in a stirring battle with Tim Gajser, as well
as Cairoli, in 2016, a big crash at Matterley Basin put
paid to his chances of defending the crown. Never
quite the same force on Yamaha during the 2017 &
’18 seasons while KTM dominated with Cairoli and
new arrival Jeffrey Herlings, an early-season injury
held him back in 2019 before he came back to take
his final GP win in blue at Loket that season.

had shown by riding through the pain following that
collarbone surgery.

## Pagina 136

MXGP HISTORY 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE

HISTORY OF THE FIM MOTOCROSS
WORLD CHAMPIONSHIP



The history of Motocross began in the early 20th century when the first events took place in Great Britain,
specifically during the 1920s when the first competitions known as “scrambles” took place. Once the
World War II ended, the sport started to grow from a national to an international level with the very first
team event known as Motocross des Nations, racing with 500cc machines, took place in 1947 in the
Netherlands. Although the Belgian rider Auguste Mingels was faster, the team classification prevailed,
and Great Britain claimed the first Trophy. From then on, this special event was held regularly every year,
and was mostly dominated by the British team until the end of the 1960s.

The Trophée des Nations, team event for 250cc machines, was added in 1961, and the Coupe des
Nations (for teams with 125cc motorcycles) in 1981. In 1984, the rules were changed: the three classes
would race together, each time entering three riders. Currently America is the most successful Nation in
this event which they have won 22 times. Back in the 50s, on the individual level, Motocross started to
grow fast, with many events held in various countries. This led the FIM to introduce the first European
Championship in 1952. That maiden series was open to 500cc motorcycles and the first winner was
Belgian Victor Leloup onboard an FN machine. He was followed by August Mingels in ‘53 and ‘54, before
British riders John Draper and Leslie Archer took over. But another country was also reaching the top
in Motocross – individual and team – in those days, beside Great Britain and Belgium, that country
was Sweden.

                                                                                148

## Pagina 149

MXGP HISTORY 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE




It all came down to a mighty tussle in the heat, and
then the pouring rain, of Australia! De Wolf needed to
close in a 13-point margin on the final day, and did all
he could to stop his German rival, but as the final race
was met by monsoon conditions it was the Dutchman
who suffered a drowning motor as the rain called an
early halt to proceedings. De Wolf took the GP win as
the laps were counted back, but Simon Längenfelder,
after three straight bronze medals, held his nerve
to become the first German World Champion for 14
years, celebrated in style with a fine dive into the mud!

The Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations
returned to the USA at a new circuit for the event,
Ironman Raceway near Crawfordsville, Indiana. The
biggest Team Presentation event ever seen for the
Nations took over the town centre at Crawfordsville
with a classic American street parade that blew
everybody’s minds! The atmosphere was at fever
pitch as ever, but the result literally stayed the
same, as Team Australia defended their title with the
same trio as in 2024. The only difference was that it
was Hunter Lawrence who went through the event
unbeaten, with his brother Jett finishing a mere 1-3
in his races! With Kyle Webster again proving ample
backup on the MX2 bike, it was actually the first time
ever in the three-man Nations format’s history that the
exact same riders had won the team title for two years
running! The event moves back to Ernée in France for
what’s sure to be a monumental weekend in 2026!

The Behind The Gate series continued to impress with
insight from MXGP Commentator Paul Malin, MXGP
Presenter Lisa Leyland, and journalist Ben Rumbold,
with the riders and teams allowing the crew further
into their world than ever before, and the Inside
the Paddock feature also delivered more craziness
from the MXoN! With Paul and US counterpart
Jason Thomas adding more analysis with the MXGP
Podcast and Lisa’s continued online Paddock Talk
series, MXGP fans received more access than ever
before into the inner workings of the top of the sport!

MXGP HISTORY 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE



MOTOCROSS FACTS AND FIGURES-FROM THE ORIGINS OF THE WORLD
CHAMPIONSHIP UP TO NOW


69 years since the first World Championship

193 World Titles claimed

2317 Grand Prix’s raced

85 World Champions

287 GP winners

Maximum number of World Titles taken by one single rider: 10/STEFAN EVERTS (BEL) in 2006

Maximum number of GP’s taken by one single rider: 112/JEFFREY HERLINGS (NED) in 2025

First manufacturer to take a World Title in each class (125/250/500): SUZUKI IN 1975

• 1975 ROGER DE COSTER (BEL)
       500cc World Champion

• 1975 GASTON RAHIER (BEL)
       125cc World Champion

• 1973 ROGER DE COSTER (BEL)
       500cc World Champion

• 1972 ROGER DE COSTER (BEL)
       500cc World Champion

• 1972 JOËL ROBERT (BEL)
       250cc World Champion

• 1971 ROGER DE COSTER (BEL)
       500cc World Champion

• 1971 JOËL ROBERT (BEL)
       250cc World Champion

• 1970 JOËL ROBERT (BEL)
       250cc World Champion

First rider to take a World Title in each class (125/250/500): ERIC GEBOERS (BEL) in 1988

• 1990 500cc World Champion HONDA

• 1988 500cc World Champion HONDA

• 1987 250cc World Champion HONDA

• 1983 125cc World Champion SUZUKI

• 1982 125cc World Champion SUZUKI

First 500cc World Champion: BILL NILSSON (SWE) in 1957 on AJS

First 250cc World Champion: TORSTEN HALLMAN (SWE) in 1962 on Husqvarna

First 125cc World Champion: GASTON RAHIER (BEL) in 1975 on Suzuki

First Women’s World Champion: LIVIA LANCELOT (FRA) in 2008 on Kawasaki

Youngest GP winner ever: KEN ROCZEN won the 2009 German MX2 GP in Teutschenthal at the age of 15
years and 53 days

Youngest Red Plate Holder ever: KEN ROCZEN at the Grand Prix of the Netherlands in Valkenswaard in 2010
at the age of 15 years and 361 days

prepared for Sunday’s races.

Race One opened with Lucas Coenen snatching the
Fox Holeshot, but Jett Lawrence wasted no time
taking control, setting the tone for upcoming day.
Early crashes for Ken Roczen and Romain Febvre
reshuffled expectations, while Tomac pushed forward
and pressured Gajser for third. De Wolf again showed
why he is MX2’s standout by finishing as the top
250cc rider. Kyle Webster made one of the key rides
of the race, recovering from a first turn crash to
reach 15th. After the opening race, Australia, USA and
France were separated by only a few points.

## Pagina 193

EMX CHAMPIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE

2025 EMXOPEN CHAMPION
CEDRIC GROBBEN





BELGIAN RIDER CEDRIC GROBBEN ENJOYED
                                       RIDER PROFILETHE WEEKEND OF HIS LIFE AT LOMMEL, WHERE
HE CLINCHED THE 2025 EMXOPEN EUROPEAN
                                               NATIONALITY                  BELGIANTITLE IN FRONT OF HIS HOME CROWD.
COMING INTO THE EVENT WITH NO MAJOR                                           RACING NUMBER                      224
EXPECTATIONS, GROBBEN KEPT HIS FOCUS
SIMPLE. RIDE SMOOTH, STAY CONSISTEN AND      AGE                                   29
MAKE THE MOST OF THE OPPORTUNITY. THAT
APPROACH PAID OFF IN THE BEST POSSIBLE        RACING NUMBER                       50
WAY.

In the opening race he quickly moved past early         BEST RESULTS:
leader Tom Guyon to take control, crossing the finish
line more than 13 seconds ahead of Guyon. In race        2025     EMXOPEN EUROPEAN CHAMPION
two, Grobben rode smart rather than risky. Climbing
steadily through the pack to finish third behind the
Baltic duo Mairis Pumpurs and Gert Krestinov. That
1–3 score was enough to secure the overall victory
and the European crown.

Grobben stated: "Unbelievable. I couldn’t even dream
about this. The second race was a little bit harder,
I didn’t get a good start and on the first lap I was a
bit nervous because of the golden plate. After a few
laps I found my flow and got back to second, but the
first guy was gone and then Krestinov passed me. He
was just a little bit too fast and at the end I was dead,
physically done. It’s just unbelievable that I won the
championship, it’s really a dream come true!"



                                                                                193

## Pagina 194

EMX CHAMPIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE




Racing in the familiar deep sand of Lommel, Cedric
showed his comfort and experience on one of the
toughest tracks in Europe. He called it “the best race
win of my career” and it’s easy to see why. His pace
was sharp, his lines were clean and he handled the
heavy conditions with ease and precision.

What makes Grobben’s success stand out even more
is his background. Unlike many riders on the EMX
grid, Cedric works a regular job during the week.
Balancing full-time work with training and racing is
no small task and that commitment often means
tough choices. One of those being skipping the
Dutch Masters this season, because he doesn’t have
enough time to train.

The EMXOpen class has long been known as a mix of
ambitious privateers and experienced national level
riders and Cedric Grobben’s 2025 campaign was the
perfect example of how far passion and persistence
can take you.





                                                                                194

THE WEEKEND IN LOKET, CZECH
REPUBLIC, WAS ONE TO REMEMBER FOR         RIDER PROFILE

DOMINANT AND COMPOSED PERFORMANCE       AGE                                   14

HANDLED THE PRESSURE OF A SINGLE-ROUND                                           RACING NUMBER                      282

                                     BEST RESULTSFrom the very first race, Herzogenrath made his

                                                 2025 EMX85 EUROPEAN CHAMPIONbuilt a lead, riding clean and smooth as the track

patiently mastered the tough Loket track, avoiding
mistakes while others struggled in the ruts. A
controlled third-place finish was enough to secure the
overall win and the European crown.

“It’s incredible! In the first race I got the holeshot and
took the win, which felt amazing. In the second race,
I started second and made my way to third on a really
difficult track. I was also a bit more stressed and didn’t
want to make the same mistake as in the Junior World
Championship in Romagné! I’m so happy,
I am European Champion, yes!" cheered
Herzogenrath after race.



                                                                                197

## Pagina 198

EMX CHAMPIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE





Beyond the results, Herzogenrath’s performance
highlighted his development as a complete rider.
His combination of speed, consistency and mental
toughness marks him as one of the brightest talents
in European youth motocross. Already a standout
on the national stage with strong results in his home
country, he continues to rise through the ranks as one
of France’s most promising young riders.

Off the track, Enzo comes from a supportive family.
His father has spoken openly about balancing
ambition with safety. Not just chasing results but
building a sustainable and responsible career. Enzo
would train mid week, balance school and attend
specialized motocross camps in the off-season
to improve. Despite his young age, Enzo already
demonstrates a mature mindset combining strong
racing instincts with technical skill.

With his 2025 title, Enzo Herzogenrath has firmly
established himself among the top prospects in
European motocross. If this performance is anything
to go by, his journey through the EMX ranks is only
just beginning.

## Pagina 199

EMX CHAMPIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE

2025 EMX65 EUROPEAN CHAMPION
MATHYS AGULLO





MATHYS AGULLO WROTE HIS NAME INTO
EUROPEAN MOTOCROSS HISTORY WITH A         RIDER PROFILE
FLAWLESS PERFORMANCE AT THE EMX65
EUROPEAN FINALS IN LOKET. SHOWING           AGE                                   11
MATURITY AND PRECISION WELL BEYOND HIS
                                               NATIONALITY                 FRENCHAGE, THE YOUNG FRENCHMAN DELIVERED TWO
NEAR-PERFECT RACES TO SECURE THE 2025                                           RACING NUMBER                      355
EMX65 EUROPEAN CHAMPION TITLE.

The weekend began with a statement in Race one. As
the gate dropped, Agullo launched into the lead with     ACHIEVEMENTS
the Fox Holeshot, immediately placing himself at the
front of the pack. The opening laps were intense, filled     EMX65 EUROPEAN CHAMPION
with pressure from riders behind and the challenges of
                                          EMX65 WORLD VICE-CHAMPION
the Czech hard-pack circuit. Despite a small tip-over
early in the race, Agullo quickly recovered, regained
control and continued to charge. His pace, focus and
ability to stay calm under pressure allowed him to
rebuild his advantage and take the first race win.

Race two showcased the same determination. After
settling into the top positions during the opening lap,
Agullo made a decisive pass on Eleu Jose around lap
two. Once in the lead, he never looked back. Lap by
lap, he extended his advantage and pulled away from
the field. When he crossed the finish line, Agullo had
secured a dominant 1–1 result with a perfect 50-point
score at the European Finals.


                                                                                199

## Pagina 200

EMX CHAMPIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE





This double-victory earned Mathys Agullo the 2025
EMX65 European Champion title, marking a major
milestone in his racing career. His strong starts
and ability to handle pressure were key ingredients
in his success at one of the most important youth
motocross events of the year.

After the race, Agullo expressed both pride and
gratitude, crediting the achievement to consistent
riding, solid starts and the people who supported him.
“I had a good weekend, a really good weekend. I rode
well, I did what I had to do, I made good starts and I'm
really happy to be European Champion, that's what I
wanted. It's a shame with the Junior World Champion,
but today I'm a European champion, which is great.
I want to thank my parents, my dad, my mum, my
coach Cyril and all my other sponsors" stated Agullo.

With his gold medal performance in Loket, Mathys
Agullo has established himself as one of the standout
young riders in European motocross. His speed,
composure and professionalism signal a promising
future and the motocross world will undoubtedly
watch his next steps closely.

MOTORCYCLES AND CLASSES

a EMXOpen motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, over 175cc up to 500cc for 2-stroke and over 175cc up to
          450cc for 4-stroke engines

b EMX2t    motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, over 175cc and up to 250cc for 2-stroke engines

c EMX250  motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, 2-stroke and 4-stroke, over 175cc and up to 250cc

d EMX125  motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, up to 125cc for 2-stroke engines

e EMX85   motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, up to 85cc for 2-stroke engines

f EMX65   motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, over 50cc and up to 65cc for 2-stroke engines
          Minimum weight 53kg, rear wheel max.12 inch, front wheel max. 14 inch only spoke wheels

AGE LIMITS

FIM EUROPE Motocross European Championship licenses for riders are issued, only when the rider has attained
the minimum age, as indicated below:

                                             MINIMUM AGE            MAXIMUM AGE

Motocross European Championship, EMX250            14 years                  21
Motocross European Championship, EMXOpen           16 years                  50
Motocross European Championship, EMX2t (2 stroke)     15 years                  50
Motocross European Championship, EMX125 (2 stroke)    13 years                  17
Motocross European Championship, EMX85 (2stroke)      11 years                  14
Motocross European Championship, EMX65 (2stroke)      8 years                   12
Women's European Championship                     14 years 250cc/13 years 125  50
Women’s Motocross of European Nations               14 years 250cc/13 years 125  50
Motocross of European Nations – EMX2 class rider        14 years                  21

The maximum age finishes at the end of the             event).
calendar year in which the rider reaches the

EMX250: motorcycles of Cat. I, Group A1, over 175cc     of 12 for EMX65, 14 for EMX85, 16/50 MX OPEN,
and up to 250cc for 2-stroke and 4-stroke engines.    17 for EMX125 2-stroke, 21 for EMX250, and 21 for
                                                    the EMX250 rider in the Motocross of European

TECHNICAL

• Riders can use 2 motorcycles during the weekend      • Sound control as per 2014 FIM Technical rules
  which have been controlled at technical verification.        for Motocross.
  In the case of a rider having 2 motorcycles, they
  must be the same make, type, cubic capacity,       • Failure of post-race sound control will be penalised
  etc.                                             by the loss of 10 positions in the race.

• The final choice of the motorcycle to be used in a
  race must be made 10 minutes before the start.
  Riders cannot change motorcycles during a Race.

## Pagina 212

EMX REGULATIONS 2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE



POINTS SCALE FOR EMX CHAMPIONSHIP RACES (ALL CLASSES)

25 points to the 1st        15 points to the 6th        10 points to the 11th        5 points to the 16th
22 points to the 2nd       14 points to the 7th         9 points to the 12th        4 points to the 17th
20 points to the 3rd        13 points to the 8th         8 points to the 13th        3 points to the 18th
18 points to the 4th        12 points to the 9th         7 points to the 14th        2 points to the 19th
16 points to the 5th        11 points to the 10th        6 points to the 15th        1 point to the 20th

WOMEN’S EMX

The championship is open to women of a minimum     FORMAT OF THE EVENT
age of 14 years old riding motorcycles from 175cc
and up to 250cc 4-stroke and 2-stroke, or 13 years      2 races of 20 minutes plus 2 laps plus normal free,
old for 125cc 2-stroke.                                timed practice and warm-up.

The event is open to any rider from any Continent,
not just Europe.





NOTES: FOR ALL CLASSES AT ALL EVENTS

The penalty for violation of the waved yellow and/or medical flags will be the loss of 10 positions from the
practise session or race in question, for each violation. Such an action will be considered as a statement
of fact to which no protests are possible. This applies to all European Championship classes and events
whether non combined events, combined with MXGP.
Proposal to reduce penalties for failed sound control from 5 positions to 2 positions for FIM event and from
10 positions to 5 positions for FIM Europe.

Whenever several flags are displayed in the same area, the superior flag always takes precedence over
the other flag(s).

At events combined with MXGP, riders must come to a complete stop if using the goggle lane to change/
replace goggles and/or gloves. Signalling is forbidden from the goggle lane. Signalling to riders can only
be done in the pit lane.
